| the bytes on disk, the plain file | 376,933,328 | 376,935,096 |
| the bytes a row takes, the original | 40.48 | 40.48 |
| the bytes a row takes, the file the probe wrote | 40.48 | 40.48 |
| the bytes a row takes, the plain file | 62.82 | 62.82 |
| the plain file's bytes over the dictionary file's | 1.55 | 1.55 |
| the plain file's write over the dictionary file's | 1.08 | 1.08 |
| the plain file's read over the dictionary file's | 2.60 | 2.62 |

**And the batch size decides the bytes.** In the first column the file the probe wrote
**is the published file, byte for byte** — **242,859,461** bytes, where the set the bump replaces had
**242,857,841**, compared byte for byte and found equal. In the second column it is **242,857,817** bytes,
where that set had **242,856,197**, and the two differ **from byte 7** — which is the third byte of the
first page header, the page's uncompressed size.

**The mechanism is in the writer.** The Parquet writer splits **every batch it is handed** into
mini-batches of `DEFAULT_WRITE_BATCH_SIZE` — **1,024** values — and the dictionary's fallback to plain, the
point where the dictionary page crosses its one-megabyte limit, is decided at one of those boundaries. **65,536
is 64 × 1,024**, so the boundaries land on the same value indices every time and the file comes out the same.
**125,000 is 122 × 1,024 + 192**, so they drift: the fallback lands later, and the file the probe wrote is
**1,644 bytes** shorter — **242,857,817** bytes against the published file's **242,859,461**. **So the
writer's output is a function of the caller's batch size**, and a store that promises reproducible files has
to fix it.

**And the encoding is the larger decision.** The same rows written with the dictionary off take **376,934,192**
bytes against **242,859,461** — where the set the bump replaces had **376,933,328** against **242,857,841** —
**1.55×** the bytes — and cost **1843.395 ms** to write against **1679.362 ms**, where that set had
**1989.261 ms** against **1844.195 ms**, so **1.10×** the write time. **And they read back 2.47× slower**,
where the set the bump replaces had **2.60×**: **1291.967 ms** against **522.671 ms** for the whole file, where
that set had **1427.515 ms** against **548.445 ms**. §3h measured the dictionary's trade on the **read** side and found it a measurement rather than a
rule; here it is measured on **both** sides, and the dictionary wins on the read by more than it loses on the
write.

**And the commit is cheap.** The whole five-step sequence — the `fsync` of the data file, the manifest
written and `fsync`ed, the `rename`, the `fsync` of the directory — is **8.493 ms**, where the set the bump
replaces had **7.651 ms**, which is **0.005** of the write. **So the protocol's shape is not constrained by
the flush on this host**, and the store's commit can be the simple one: the data durable first, then the
manifest that names it, then the `rename`, then the directory.

**And the `fsync` measured here is the platform's `fsync`, not a drive's flush.** On APFS the two are
different: `fsync` makes the file's data durable with respect to the operating system, while `F_FULLFSYNC`
additionally flushes the drive's write cache. Rust's `File::sync_all` is the former. The **2.480 ms** above is
therefore the **logical** flush's cost, and the durability it buys on this platform is weaker than the number
suggests. **The commit protocol has to be checked on the platform it will ship on.**

**And the four decisions the numbers make.** The **row groups** are a million rows — the file's own — and one
of them costs **279.785 ms** to write, where the set the bump replaces had **306.230 ms**. The **commit** is a
manifest commit of five steps at **8.493 ms**, where that set had **7.651 ms**, and nothing in its shape is
forced by the flush here. The **encoding** keeps the dictionary on: **35.6%** fewer bytes and **2.47×** faster
to read for **1.10×** the write time, where the set the bump replaces had **2.60×** faster to read for
**1.08×** the write time. And the **batch size** is fixed at **65,536**, because the bytes depend on it.

**And the answer to the question the store's premise rests on.** The write costs **3.22×** a full read of the
file, where the set the bump replaces had **3.36×**, and **23.66×** a two-column read of it, where that set
had **24.04×**. So *write once, read many* pays for itself after about **four** full reads, or about
**twenty-four** scans. **For a bulk-loaded analytical table that is read many times, it does** — and the read
side of this repository is the half that is measured to a tenth of a millisecond.

**And why the write moved is the writer, not the host.** The write of one row group is **282.134 ms**, where
the set the bump replaces had **313.043 ms**, and the write of the whole file is **1679.362 ms** against
**1844.195 ms** — **faster**, on a host that was **busier**: the load averages were **12.74**, **10.86** and
**9.31** for the first set and **10.52**, **10.90** and **10.84** for the second, where the set the bump
replaces' were **7.87**, **7.57** and **7.99** and **9.63**, **7.64** and **9.00**. **What changed between
the two sets is the writer** — the `parquet` crate is at **58.4.1** now, and its output is different: the file
it wrote is **242,859,461** bytes where the set the bump replaces' was **242,857,841**. **And the commit is
the other way**: **8.493 ms** against **7.651 ms**, and it is the flushes that carry it — **2.480 ms**,
**2.451 ms** and **2.508 ms** against **2.118 ms**, **2.382 ms** and **2.587 ms**. **The flush is the host's,
and the write is the writer's.**

The raw reports are committed under their own names — the set the bump replaces under
`bench-results/write-cost-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json` and
`bench-results/write-cost-engine-batch-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json` — and the figures above are derived
from them by `python3 scripts/medians.py`; the guard checks every one of them **inside this section**. The
whole old-and-new is in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

### 3t. The store's own write

**The write side is measured, and the store is built.** Spec 038 built the store's half — the **writer**,
the **manifest**, the **commit** and the **catalog** — and the acceptance is the strongest one available:
**the store wrote the published file, byte for byte**.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the file the store wrote** | **242,859,461 bytes — the published file, byte for byte** |
| the rows | 6,000,000 |
| the grid the rows arrived in | 65,536 |
| the row groups | 1,000,000 |
| | |
| the store's write | **1720.392 ms** |
| the store's commit | **8.043 ms** |
| the store's own API, end to end | **1721.022 ms** |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same store, the same rows, against the tree the
bump replaces:

| | |
|---|---|
| **the file the store wrote** | **242,857,841 bytes — the published file, byte for byte** |
| the rows | 6,000,000 |
| the grid the rows arrived in | 65,536 |
| the row groups | 1,000,000 |
| | |
| the store's write | **1793.670 ms** |
| the store's commit | **9.270 ms** |
| the store's own API, end to end | **1798.356 ms** |

**And it is the same write that [§3s](#3s-the-write-side) measured.** The store's write of the published
file is **1720.392 ms**, where the set the bump replaces had **1793.670 ms**, and the write-side probe's is
**1679.362 ms**, where that set had **1844.195 ms**: the same writer over the same rows, on two days, within
a few per cent of each other. **Which is why the load average travels with every figure in this file**: the
store's campaigns ran at a load average of **10.77**, **10.31** and **9.75**, where the set the bump
replaces' were **8.4**, **8.48** and **8.32**, and the probe's at **12.74**, **10.86** and **9.31**, where that
set had **7.87**, **7.57** and **7.99**.

**And the store's commit is the five steps, measured.** The data file is made durable first, then the
manifest that names it is written, made durable, and `rename`d over the old one, and the directory is made
durable last. **8.043 ms**, where the set the bump replaces had **9.270 ms**, against the probe's **8.493 ms**
for the same protocol, where that set had **7.651 ms**. **There is no write-ahead log and no rollback**: a
commit is a rename, and the rename is the answer.

**And the grid is a rule now, not a surprise.** The rows arrive in batches of **65,536** rows and the store
**checks** it: a batch off the grid is refused with an error, because a file that comes out differently is
not a bug in the store but a caller that batched differently — and the store would rather say so than write
a file whose bytes nobody can predict. **That is the finding of [§3s](#3s-the-write-side) turned into a
contract**, and the byte-identical file above is the contract holding.

**And what the store is not, is named.** **No SQL. No transactions with rollback. No MVCC, no snapshot
isolation. No concurrent writers. No row-level updates. No compaction. One writer.** A table grows by
appending a file and committing it; it does not shrink, and it does not change in place.

**And the store sits below the engine.** `columnar-store` does not depend on `columnar-engine`: it writes
the files the engine reads, and the engine's read path cannot move because of anything here. **The glue
between them is a path** — and the engine's side of that glue, the source that reads a table which is
**many** files, is the next increment.

The raw reports are committed under their own names — the set the bump replaces under
`bench-results/store-write-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json` — the figures above are derived from them by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks every one of them **inside this section**. The whole
old-and-new is in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

### 3u. The file boundary's cost

**The store writes a table as a set of files, and the engine's only source is one file.** So the question
is what the **file boundary** costs, before the engine is taught to read a table that is many files.

**The experiment is the published file's own six row groups**, because they are the same rows as the
published file: the probe writes them as **six files**, and then reads **the one file** and **the six
files**, through the engine's own reader, in the same campaign, on the same host. **The only difference
between the two reads is the number of files.**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the read of the one file** | **70.151 ms** |
| **the read of the six files** | **68.267 ms** |
| **the boundary's cost** | **−1.416 ms** |
| the boundary's share of the one file's read | **−0.020** |
| the files | 6 |
| the rows | 6,000,000 |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same probe, the same six files, against the tree
the bump replaces:

| | |
|---|---|
| **the read of the one file** | **66.003 ms** |
| **the read of the six files** | **64.114 ms** |
| **the boundary's cost** | **−1.152 ms** |
| the boundary's share of the one file's read | **−0.017** |
| the files | 6 |
| the rows | 6,000,000 |

**And the two are level.** The read of the one file is **70.151 ms**, where the set the bump replaces had
**66.003 ms**, and the read of the six files is **68.267 ms**, where that set had **64.114 ms**; the
boundary's cost is **−1.416 ms**, where that set had **−1.152 ms**, and its share of the one file's read is
**−0.020**, where that set had **−0.017**. **The difference is negative, so the six files were not slower:
the file boundary costs nothing measurable.** And the two reads' own iterations overlap — the one file's
span **68.768–72.085 ms** and the six files' **66.713–71.364 ms**. And the rows are the same: the probe
compares them **row by row, in order**, on the key column, and they match.

**And the host was busier.** The campaigns were taken at a load average of **13.84**, **13.84** and **12.97**,
where the set the bump replaces' were **3.48**, **3.44** and **3.88** — and the absolute times moved with them.
**The boundary's cost did not**: it is **−1.416 ms**, where that set had **−1.152 ms** — negative in both, and
the claim does not depend on the absolute times.

**And so the source's case is correctness, not performance.** The naive way to read a store's table — open
each file, run the query over each one — is **wrong three times over**: the rows of a filtered table are
split across the files, the groups of an aggregate are split across them, and the sides of a join are split
across them. **A source that reads the set as one table is what makes that right**, and the measurement
above says what it has to achieve on the performance side: **nothing**. The boundary is free, so the source
**may not cost anything either** — its whole case is that it is correct.

The raw reports are committed under their own names — the set the bump replaces under
`bench-results/many-files-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json` — the figures above are derived from them by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks every one of them **inside this section**. The whole
old-and-new is in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).
### 3v. The source over a set of files

**The boundary is free, so the engine gained a source that reads the set as one table** — `FileSetSource`,
one new name on the surface — **and the question is what that source costs, and whether it is right.**

**The experiment is the same one §3u used**: the published file's own six row groups, written as **six
files**, and read **three ways** in the same campaign, on the same host — **the one file**, **the six files
one by one**, and **the six files as one table**, the last through the engine's new source. **The rows are
the same, the columns are the same, and the only difference is how the files are read.** **The set this
file publishes is `bench-results/file-set-run{1,2,3}.json`; the set the bump replaces is
`bench-results/file-set-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json`, and both are committed.**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the read of the one file** | **70.349 ms** |
| **the read of the six files, one by one** | **67.332 ms** |
| **the read of the six files, as one table** | **69.532 ms** |
| **the source's cost over the one file's read** | **−0.991 ms** |
| the source's cost, as a share of that read | **−0.014** |
| the parallel read of the one file | **35.879 ms** |
| the parallel read of the six, as one table | **35.920 ms** |
| the source's cost, in parallel | **−0.019 ms** |
| the source's cost, in parallel, as a share | **−0.001** |
| the open of the one file | **0.052 ms** |
| the open of the six files | **0.166 ms** |
| the open of the six over the open of the one | **0.113 ms** |
| the set over the six files read one by one | **1.886 ms** |
| **the boundary's cost, as §3u measured it** | **−3.056 ms** |
| the files | 6 |
| the rows | 6,000,000 |
| the filtered count, over the file and over the set | 3,184,637 |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same code, on `parquet` 54.3.1, measured
before the dependency moved:

| | |
|---|---|
| **the read of the one file** | **68.721 ms** |
| **the read of the six files, one by one** | **65.846 ms** |
| **the read of the six files, as one table** | **67.400 ms** |
| **the source's cost over the one file's read** | **−0.599 ms** |
| the source's cost, as a share of that read | **−0.009** |
| the parallel read of the one file | **35.123 ms** |
| the parallel read of the six, as one table | **35.399 ms** |
| the source's cost, in parallel | **0.133 ms** |
| the source's cost, in parallel, as a share | **0.004** |
| the open of the one file | **0.064 ms** |
| the open of the six files | **0.187 ms** |
| the open of the six over the open of the one | **0.120 ms** |
| the set over the six files read one by one | **2.010 ms** |
| **the boundary's cost, as §3u measured it** | **−2.874 ms** |
| the files | 6 |
| the rows | 6,000,000 |
| the filtered count, over the file and over the set | 3,184,637 |

**And the source is free.** The source's cost over the one file's read is **−0.991 ms** — **negative**,
where it was **−0.599 ms** in the set the bump replaces — and a share of **−0.014**: **the set's read
was the faster of the two in all three campaigns**, by **1.170 ms**, **0.536 ms** and **0.991 ms**. In
parallel it is **−0.019 ms** against **35.879 ms**, a share of **−0.001**, where it was **0.133 ms** and
**0.004**, inside the parallel read's own spread of **35.813–36.074 ms**. **So the sign changed and the
claim did not.**

**And one claim of the set the bump replaces does not hold here.** There the difference sat inside the
one file's own spread of campaigns — **67.998–69.901 ms**; here the three campaigns' medians sit in
**70.288–70.523 ms**, and the difference of **0.991 ms** is wider than that spread. **What carries the
claim now is the sign**: the set's read was the faster one in every campaign, and a source that costs
less than nothing costs nothing.

**So reading a table that is many files costs nothing measurable over reading the same rows in one file**,
and the source's whole case — that it is *correct* — costs nothing.

**And the open is nothing either.** Six files cost **0.166 ms** against one file's **0.052 ms**: the five
extra footers are **0.113 ms**. In the set the bump replaces they were **0.187 ms**, **0.064 ms** and
**0.120 ms**.

**And the set costs 1.886 ms over the files read one by one** — where it cost **2.010 ms** in the set the
bump replaces — and that is the price of **making one table out of six reads**. The naive way's read is
not the same answer: it produces six answers, and the rows of a filtered table are split across them.
**The yardstick is the one file's read**, and against it the source is free.

**And the answers are the engine's.** The probe compares the set's rows with the one file's **row by row,
in order**, on the key column, and they match; and the same filtered plan over the file and over the set
counts the same **3,184,637** rows.

**And the one file's read moved, and the source's cost over it stayed negative.** The one file's own read
is **70.349 ms** where it was **68.721 ms** in the set the bump replaces — the set this file publishes
was taken on a busier host (the load average **14.06**, **14.06** and **13.66**, against **3.94**,
**4.41** and **12.31**) and on the regenerated file. `execute_on` takes a `ParquetSource` exactly as it
did, and the engine's internal source trait delegates to the file's own methods — **the engine's own
code did not change** — and **what this section is about did not move with the read**: the source's cost
over it is **−0.991 ms**, still negative, and in parallel **−0.019 ms**. The source is **additive**: a
new name on the surface, not a change to the old one.

The raw reports are committed under their own names, the figures above are derived from them by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks every one of them **inside this section**. **And the
whole old-and-new is in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).**

### 3w. The reader's four gigabytes

**A library is read by people who did not write it, and it reads files its users provide** — so what a
hostile file can ask of the reader is not a detail, it is the trust boundary. **The published record
said a hostile file can ask for four gigabytes; this is the measurement of what it asks, and the
hole is closed.**

**The hostile file is a real file with one hostile page header.** The probe writes a real Parquet
file — one column, one row group, one page — with `parquet`'s own writer, and then replaces that
page's header with a hand-written one: a Thrift compact header whose statistics field **declares a
length** and then stops. **The footer is untouched**, so the file's metadata still describes it and
the reader walks it exactly as it walks an honest one. **The declared length is a curve** — 1 MiB,
64 MiB, 1 GiB, 4 GiB — and **an honest file is the control**, so the figure says what the reader
asks for when there is nothing hostile in it.

**The same probe, the same hostile file, on the tree before the bump and on the tree this file
ships.** The set before the bump is `hostile-metadata-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json`, the set this
file publishes is `hostile-metadata-run{1,2,3}.json`, and both are committed.

| the declared length | the largest allocation asked for, before | the largest allocation asked for, now | the share of the declared length, now |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1 MiB** | **1,048,576** | **8,192** | **0.0078125** |
| **64 MiB** | **67,108,864** | **8,192** | **0.0001221** |
| **1 GiB** | **1,073,741,824** | **8,192** | **0.0000076** |
| **4 GiB** | **4,294,967,295** | **8,192** | **0.0000019** |

| | before the bump | this tree ships |
|---|---|---|
| **the share of the declared length the reader asked for** | **1.0** | **0.0000019** — at the top of the curve |
| **the largest allocation, for an honest file** | **8,192** | **8,192** |
| the hostile header's size | 25 bytes | 25 bytes |
| the read's time, for the declared 1 MiB | 0.076 ms | **0.079 ms** |
| the read's time, for the declared 64 MiB | 0.059 ms | **0.043 ms** |
| the read's time, for the declared 1 GiB | 0.062 ms | **0.046 ms** |
| the read's time, for the declared 4 GiB | 0.089 ms | **0.044 ms** |
| the honest file's read time | 0.300 ms | **0.095 ms** |
| what the reader said | `end of file` | `failed to fill whole buffer` |

**And the reader no longer asks for what the file declares — it asks for what an honest file
costs.** Before the bump the largest single allocation **was** the declared length at every point of
the curve, a share of **1.0**, out of a **25-byte** header: a file of a few hundred bytes made the
reader ask for **4,294,967,295** bytes. **Now it asks for 8,192 at every declared length** — the
control's own figure, the same at 1 MiB and at 4 GiB — so the share of the declared length is
**0.0000019** at the top of the curve, two parts in a billion.

**And the read's own time moved with the reader.** The declared 1 MiB read was **0.076 ms** in the
set the bump replaces and is **0.079 ms** in this one; the 64 MiB read was **0.059 ms** and is
**0.043 ms**; the 1 GiB read was **0.062 ms** and is **0.046 ms**; and the 4 GiB read was
**0.089 ms** and is **0.044 ms**. The honest file's own read was **0.300 ms** and is **0.095 ms**.

**And the cost was address space, not time, and it still is.** The old reader's 4 GiB read took
**0.089 ms**, *less* than the honest file's own **0.300 ms**, because four gigabytes of untouched
pages cost the reader almost nothing to ask for and everything to carry. The reader this file ships
takes **0.044 ms** for the same file.

**And the hole was in the page headers, not in the metadata.** The record said the *metadata*: a
hostile file's footer declaring a four-gigabyte string. **It does not** — `parquet` reads the
footer through its own **slice** protocol, which asks the slice for the declared range and refuses
it when the bytes are not there, *before* it allocates anything, so the largest allocation made
while opening a hostile footer is **8 KiB**, the same as an honest file's. **The page headers are the
reachable path**: they are read one after another as the reader walks a row group, through thrift's
**streaming** compact protocol, and there `read_bytes` does `vec![0u8; len]` — it allocates the
declared length before it reads a byte of it.

**And the fix the record named would not have closed it.** `thrift` ≥ 0.23 refuses an over-long
declared length **only if the caller asks it to**: the limit is `max_string_size`, and the default
configuration sets it to **none**. `parquet` sets no limit either. **What closes it is `parquet`'s
own reader**: from **58.4.1** it does not read the page statistics by default, so the hostile
declared length is never parsed at all — **and that is the whole of the fix**: the engine adds no
wrapper, no bound and no check of its own, because the reader is where the hole was and the reader
is where the fix is. The bump is to the **smallest** version that closes it — the 59 and 60 lines
drop `thrift` outright, which is a bigger jump than the evidence asks for — and it is in the
workspace's shared dependency table, so it cannot drift between crates. The figures it moved are in
[§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

The raw reports are committed under their own names, the figures above are derived from them by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks every one of them **inside this section**.

---

### 3x. The bump, and the figures it moved

**What this section is**: the headline figure of every family this increment touched, **old and new,
side by side**. The sections above publish each figure in the place that owns it, with the figure
it replaces beside it; this is the one place where they are all together, so that a reader who
wants to know *what the bump moved* does not have to read the whole file to find out.

**What moved, and why, in four sentences.**

1. **The bump changed the writer's bytes**, so the dataset was **regenerated** — `data/lineitem.parquet`
   is **242,859,461** bytes where it was 242,857,841, and `data/orders.parquet` is 49,303,806 where
   it was 49,303,490. The data pages are identical; the metadata differs. **A regenerated dataset
   moves every figure that reads a file**, which is why the campaign is the whole set and not the
   families the bump was expected to touch.
2. **The engine's cold and load paths read the file through `parquet`'s reader**, and 58.4.1 is a
   different reader from 54.3.1. Those are the figures the bump moved *directly*.
3. **The incumbents do not read the file through this repository's Arrow** — `DuckDB`, `Polars` and
   the `DataFusion` peer have their own readers — so **their** movement is the machine's, and they
   are the **yardstick** this file has always used for that.
4. **The machine's own floor was measured in the same campaign**, so the host's movement can be
   told apart from the code's: the floor the join probe measures with no engine in it went from
   **6.147 ms** to **4.948 ms**, and every system moved with it.

| the family | the figure | the set the bump replaces | this file publishes |
|---|---|---|---|
| the comparison | the engine's cold `q6`, lazy | 15.4 | **14.9** |
| the comparison | the engine's cold `q6`, eager | 552.3 | **587.2** |
| the comparison | `DuckDB`'s cold `q6` — **the yardstick** | 32.0 | **35.0** |
| the comparison | the engine's resident `q6` | 9.4 | **10.1** |
| the query probe | the whole query, resident (`join-orders`) | 21.324 | **21.802** |
| the sort probe | the stage, cold | 1.10 | **1.68** |
| the join probe | the stage, cold | 24.690 | **27.493** |
| the join probe | the floor the probe — **the machine's own measure** | 6.147 | **4.948** |
| the write probe | the write of the whole file | 1,844.195 | **1,679.362** |
| the store's probe | the commit, all five steps | 9.270 | **8.043** |
| the materialised column | Arrow's decode | 6.09 | **4.56** |
| the RLE expansion | the expansion | 1.79 | **1.77** |
| the dictionary probe | the crate's read, expanded, 6 groups | 15.46 | **14.30** |
| the row-group probe | the derived batch, 6 groups | 15.32 | **14.73** |
| the sweep | the engine at 1 thread, 6 groups | 15.4 | **14.6** |
| the curve | the engine at 6M rows | 57.7 | **47.9** |
| the curve | the engine at 24M rows | 232.2 | **193.6** |
| the kernel | the Rust SIMD over the scalar | 2.44 | **2.46** |
| the hostile file | the largest allocation, for a declared 4 GiB | 4,294,967,295 | **8,192** |

**Read the table with the four sentences above, and not as nineteen verdicts.** The engine's cold
`q6` is 15.4 ms in the set the bump replaces and 14.9 ms here, and **DuckDB's is 32.0 ms and
35.0 ms** — the engine got faster and the yardstick got slower, on a machine whose own floor got
19% faster. The eager path went the other way, 552.3 ms to 587.2 ms, and **that movement is the
host's**: the eager path *is* the decode, and the decode moved with the host, with the incumbents
beside it — [§2b-bis](#2b-bis-the-eager-path-got-faster-too-and-it-is-measured) has the argument.
**The bump's own figure is the one the host's hand cannot move**: the lazy path's cold `q6`,
which reads the file through the new Arrow. The **hostile file's** row is the only one that is not
a timing at all: the reader's largest allocation at a declared four gigabytes fell from the
declared length to **8,192** bytes, and that is the whole of this increment — see
[§3w](#3w-the-readers-four-gigabytes).

**The curve's two rows are the host's, and the incumbents say so.** The engine's 6M figure is 47.9 ms
where the set the bump replaces has 57.7 ms, and its 24M figure is 193.6 ms against 232.2 ms — but
**DuckDB's moved too**, 69.0 ms to 61.0 ms and 268.0 ms to 244.0 ms, and DuckDB does not read the
file through this repository's Arrow. That campaign ran on a quieter host — the load average is in
the report — and what holds is the comparison: the engine is ahead of every incumbent at every
size, in both sets. See [§3i](#3i-the-curve-over-the-size-of-the-data).

**And no figure here is a verdict about the engine on its own.** A figure that moved in the same
direction as the yardstick moved with the host; a figure that moved against it did not. That is why
the yardstick is in the table, and why the machine's own floor is in it too.

**There was no interleaved old-code A/B, and the reason is the yardstick.** An A/B would need both
trees' binaries measured against each other on the same machine inside the same session, which this
repository has not done for a dependency bump; **spec 034's precedent** is the one this increment
follows — the incumbents, which do not read the file through this repository's Arrow, are the
host's yardstick, and the machine's own floor is measured in the same campaign as the figures it is
compared against. **The superseded sets are committed under their own names**
(`bench-results/<family>-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json`), so the column on the left of every pair
above is derivable from a committed report and not from a memory.

**And the store's acceptance holds again, measured.** The store writes the published file
byte for byte — that is the store's acceptance test — and the bump changed the writer's bytes, so
the published file was **regenerated** and the acceptance re-measured on the regenerated file. The
write probe's own figure for writing the whole file is **1,679.362 ms** where it was 1,844.195 ms,
and the store's commit — all five steps, fsync data, write manifest, fsync manifest, rename,
fsync dir — is **8.043 ms** where it was 9.270 ms. See
[§3s](#3s-the-write-side) and [§3t](#3t-the-stores-own-write).

Every figure in this table is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and
checked by the guard **inside this section**, and every one of them is published in the section
that owns it as well.

### 3y. The build side takes a set

**What this section is**: the join's **build side** -- the right-hand table, the one the hash table is
built from before the probe looks anything up -- read from **a set of files** instead of one. The
question is what the set costs, and what it does not.

**The experiment.** The published file's own **six row groups** are written as **six files** -- the
experiment spec 039 already used -- and the build side is built twice in the same campaign: once from
the **one file**, and once from the **six files**. The probe side is the same in both, so the only thing
that moves is the build side. The build side is `data/lineitem.parquet` -- six million rows, six row
groups -- and the probe side is `data/orders.parquet`.

| | the one file | the six files | the difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| the table's construction | **60.956 ms** | **62.578 ms** | **+1.622 ms** |
| the build side's open | **0.141 ms** | **0.317 ms** | **+0.176 ms** |

**The set costs 1.622 ms to build, and 0.176 ms to open.** The open is six footers against one, and it
is the only part that scales with the number of files; the construction reads the same six million keys
either way. The medians are of three campaigns, and the spreads are 60.186-61.670 ms and 60.801-64.448
ms for the construction and 0.139-0.170 ms and 0.303-0.353 ms for the open. **The host's load average
in those campaigns was 8.33, 7.89 and 8.86** -- a busy host, and the pairs are measured **together**, so
the host's own movement is in both sides of every pair above.

**The identity is exact, and it was measured.** The same **1,501,215** rows come out of the join, and
the same **79,530,106,032.52** comes out of the sum -- the one file and the six files, the same answer
to the bit. A set that gave a different answer would be a different table, and the plan output's own
row count would say so.

**The table's shape does not change, and the estimate does not change with it.** The key's range is
**5,999,997** keys wide, one file and six files alike: a set's range is the **union** of the files'
ranges, and the union of a partition's parts is the partition. So the direct-addressed table fits in
both cases -- the direct table is the cheap one -- and the estimate is **103,875,021 bytes** both ways.
That is the estimate's three facts, unchanged: the rows are the sum of the files' rows, the range is the
union of the files' ranges, and the workers are the set's own.

**What this cost.** The plan's own shape: `JoinSpec.build_path` became `JoinSpec.build_paths`, a list of
paths. One path is one file, exactly as it was -- the one-file path is opened, read and charged as it
always was, and the published figures in §3 do not move. That is **one public break**, and it was made
while the crates were still unpublished, so it cost nothing. [ADR-0053](docs/adr/0053-the-build-side-takes-a-set.md)
records it.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked
by the guard **inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/build-set-run1.json`,
`bench-results/build-set-run2.json` and `bench-results/build-set-run3.json`, and the probe is
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/build_set_probe.rs`.

---

### 3z. The engine's rows reach a store's table

**What this section is**: the store's claim -- *"fed the same rows, it writes the same file, byte for
byte"* -- measured through the **engine's** rows instead of Arrow's. The engine holds its rows in its
own vocabulary, a column chunk with an encoding and a validity mask; the store writes Arrow's. Somebody
turns one into the other, and that somebody's cost and the file it produces are what this section
publishes.

**The two shapes of the engine's rows.** The **file's own rows** are the engine's eager read of the
published file -- the rows as they lie, dictionaries and all. **A plan's rows** are the rows that
survive a filter, gathered by the engine's own projection: the gather materialises the dictionary away,
so those rows are plain columns and their file is a different file by construction. Both are measured.

**Spec 044 re-measured this section, and the bytes did not move.** The reason is in one line: **a plan's
rows are still gathered**, because the Q6 plan's predicates remove rows in the engine, and the gather is
the right thing to do when they do. **The timings moved inside the noise**, and **every figure that moved
is published with the one it replaced**.

| | the cost | the bytes |
|---|---|---|
| the engine's eager read | **555.049 ms** *(was 547.734 ms)* | -- |
| the conversion: the engine's vocabulary to Arrow's | **149.271 ms** *(was 149.809 ms)* | -- |
| the store's own API, end to end | **1,825.182 ms** *(was 1,826.588 ms)* | **242,854,029** |
| the store's own Arrow path, for comparison | **2,337.143 ms** *(was 2,266.358 ms)* | **242,859,461** |
| a plan's rows: the read | **74.280 ms** *(was 76.748 ms)* | -- |
| a plan's rows: the write | **15.784 ms** *(was 15.059 ms)* | **982,089** |

The published file is **242,859,461** bytes. **The conversion is 8.2% of the write it serves** -- 149.271
ms against 1,825.182 ms -- and the engine's own rows cost **149.271 ms** to convert and the store's API
**1,825.182 ms** to write and commit them. The medians are of three campaigns; the spreads are
553.120-563.166 ms, 147.442-153.275 ms, 1,816.742-1,831.367 ms and 2,303.320-2,372.850 ms. **The host's
load average in those campaigns was 6.89, 6.94 and 6.52** -- a busy host, and the two paths are measured
**in the same campaign**, so the host's movement is in both. **The bytes did not move at all**: the eager
read keeps the dictionary, the plan's rows are gathered, and both write what they wrote.

**Three files, three questions.**

1. **The store's Arrow path writes the published file byte for byte.** That is the store's claim, and it
   is unchanged: **242,859,461** bytes, no differing byte.
2. **The store's conversion, under the published file's own schema, writes it byte for byte too.**
   **242,859,461** bytes, no differing byte. **So the engine's rows are the file's rows, and the
   conversion is exact, value for value.** The engine's dictionary -- one dictionary for the whole file,
   merged from every batch -- does not decide the bytes: the Parquet writer interns the values again,
   per row group, and the first appearance order is the rows' own order.
3. **The store's own `append_rows` writes a different file**: **242,854,029** bytes, and the first
   difference is at byte **20,566,725**. **The difference is the schema's nullability, and nothing
   else** -- 5,432 bytes of it. The published file declares two columns optional that hold no NULL, and
   the store's rule -- *a column that holds no NULL is written REQUIRED* -- writes them required. A
   required column carries no definition levels and an optional one does, and that is what the bytes
   see.

**That rule is the store's, and it is published.** The engine's rows say **which rows are NULL**; they
do not say **whether a column may be NULL**, because the engine's vocabulary does not carry it. So the
store's rule is stated rather than discovered: **a column that holds no NULL is written REQUIRED, and a
column that holds one is written OPTIONAL.** A caller who needs a schema the rows cannot carry writes
through the Arrow path, where the schema comes with the rows.

**A plan's rows are a different file by construction**: **115,821** rows in **982,089** bytes, with the
dictionary materialised away. **And that is still right after spec 044**: the gather is the right thing
to do when a plan removes rows, because the survivors are scattered through the batch and have to be
pulled out. The engine gained the way to hand a plan's rows out -- `execute_rows` and `execute_rows_on` --
and **a plan with an aggregation is refused**, because the aggregate folds the rows into groups and there
is nothing left to write. The embedder's glue is two lines: hand the engine's rows
to the store. The **grid is the store's** -- the rows are re-batched inside the store, and the engine
does not learn it.

**What this cost.** The store depends on `columnar-core` -- the vocabulary, and nothing else in this
workspace -- because the conversion cannot live in the core (a vocabulary that depends on a framework is
not a vocabulary) and must not live in the engine (the engine's surface would carry Arrow). The
dependency rule that ADR-0044, ADR-0047, `STATUS.md`, the store's manifest, its README and
`docs/embedding.md` published is **corrected in each of the places that published it**, and
[ADR-0054](docs/adr/0054-the-engines-rows-reach-a-stores-table.md) records the correction and the
decision.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked
by the guard **inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/engine-rows-run1.json`,
`bench-results/engine-rows-run2.json` and `bench-results/engine-rows-run3.json`, and the probe is
`crates/columnar-store/examples/engine_rows_probe.rs`.

---

### 3aa. The set's rows

**What this section is**: the set's **own rows** handed out, and the **round trip** -- a store's table read
by the engine and written back by the store. Spec 042 gave the engine a way to hand **a file's** rows out
(`execute_rows_on`) and the store a way to take them (`append_rows`), but **the set's own rows still
stopped at the aggregate**: `FileSetSource::execute` folded them into groups, and a store's table could
not be read by the engine and written back. Spec 043 gives the set its own door --
`FileSetSource::execute_rows` -- and this section is what that door costs and what comes out of it.

**Spec 044 moved the figures in this section, and every one of them is published with the one it
replaced.** The reason is in one line: **the rows the engine hands out are the rows that were read, when
they are**, so the gather is gone -- and [§3ab](#3ab-the-rows-as-they-lie) is that increment.

**The experiment.** The published file's own **six row groups** written as **six files** -- the experiment
spec 039 used and spec 042 reused -- and the round trip run **twice in the same campaign**: once over the
**one file**, and once over the **six files**. The rows are the same rows, so the only thing that moves is
the set.

**The cost of the set: nothing that can be claimed.**

| | the one file | the six files | the difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| the rows out | **549.167 ms** *(was 3,338.468 ms)* | **550.301 ms** *(was 3,344.893 ms)* | **+1.133 ms** *(was +6.425 ms)* |
| the write | **1,860.799 ms** *(was 2,040.668 ms)* | **1,836.466 ms** *(was 2,075.327 ms)* | **−24.333 ms** *(was +34.659 ms)* |

**The two differences are the host's, and the yardstick says so.** The two arms are measured **back to
back** in every iteration and **the order they are measured in alternates**, so the host's own movement is
in both sides of every pair -- and the difference between the arms, in every iteration, is:

| | the differences, in every iteration | their median |
|---|---|---|
| the read | +48.423, −37.925, +168.260, +23.317, −52.658, +170.690, −10.798, +0.945, +5.125 | **+5.125 ms** |
| the write | −60.948, −174.146, −27.641, −32.745, −36.371, +147.019, −54.045, +12.497, +73.197 | **−32.745 ms** |

**The read's differences straddle zero, and their spread is wider than the difference of the medians.**
The read's median (**+5.125 ms**) is **0.9% of the read** -- and their spread runs from −52.658 to
+170.690 ms, so the spread is forty-three times the median. **The host's load average in those campaigns
was 5.88, 6.69 and 7.22.** **No difference can be claimed**: the set's cost is inside the noise, which is
what §3v and §3y found twice already and what the third measurement confirms.

**And the estimate does not move either**: **1,549,500,016 bytes** for both. The rows are the sum of the
files' rows and the columns are the same, so the budget is charged the same for a set as for one file.

**The read is not a copy any more.** The rows the engine hands out are **the rows that were read**: the
plan's projection, handed out with the reader's own encodings, and **the gather is gone for a plan that
removes nothing**. The eager path separates the two, because it reads the same file and then hands the
same rows out:

| | the cost |
|---|---|
| the engine's eager read | **551.325 ms** *(was 516.196 ms)* |
| the eager rows: the copy of the columns | **154.858 ms** *(was 2,823.164 ms)* |
| the eager path's total | **706.634 ms** *(was 3,353.618 ms)* |

**The copy is 21.9% of the eager path's cost, and it was 84.2% before.** The copy is what the **eager**
path pays, because the caller owns the dataset there: the rows are a copy of the columns rather than the
columns themselves. **And the lazy path does not pay it at all**: the one file's rows cost **549.167 ms**,
which is its scan and nothing else -- the columns are **moved** out of the reader, not copied. **§3ab has
the before-and-after, and the reason the gather is gone.**

**The correction: the gather was published wrong, and it is corrected here.** The published figure was
**2,292.710 ms**, and it was the difference between the eager rows and the eager read. **But the eager
rows measurement did not include the read**: the probe measures the decode and the rows **separately**, so
the gather was the whole of the eager rows -- **2,823.164 ms** -- and the eager path's total was
**3,353.618 ms**. **The published "81%" was wrong too**: the gather was **84.2%** of the eager path's
cost, and not 81%. **The claim the wrong figure supported was right** -- the gather was most of what the
eager path spent -- **and the figure was wrong**. ADR-0056 records the correction.

**The identity is exact, and the file is the witness.** The same **6,000,000** rows come out of the one
file and out of the six files, and **the two files the two round trips write are the same file, byte for
byte**. A file is a stronger witness than a checksum, because a checksum is a summary and a file is the
thing itself: the set's rows and the one file's rows are the same rows, in the same order, with the same
values.

**The round trip is the published file, byte for byte.**

| | the bytes | the first differing byte | the data |
|---|---|---|---|
| the published file | 242,859,461 | -- | -- |
| the round trip, the store's schema rule | **242,854,029** *(was 242,853,681)* | **20,566,725** | **different** |
| the round trip, the file's own schema | **242,859,461** *(was 242,859,117)* | **no differing byte** | **the same, byte for byte** |

**One difference, and it is the store's rule.**

1. **The store's rule** -- a column that holds no NULL is written REQUIRED -- makes the definition levels
   differ, and the first difference is at byte **20,566,725**: **the same byte §3z published** for the
   one-file path, because it is the same rule and the same rows. **And the data is different**, because the
   definition levels are in the data pages. **The published file's metadata starts at 242,845,766**, so this
   difference is in the data and not in the metadata.
2. **The file's own schema removes that difference, and then there is no difference left.** The rows are the
   rows that were read, so they keep the dictionary, and the file that comes out is the file they came
   from: **242,859,461** bytes, and **no differing byte**.

**So the round trip reproduces the published file.** The Parquet writer stores the Arrow schema in the
footer, and the Arrow schema says the dictionary columns are dictionaries; **the rows are the rows that
were read, so they say the same thing**. **The Parquet schema is the same and the Arrow schema is the
same** -- both measured -- and the **344 bytes** the old round trip was short of are **gone**.

**And the first differing byte was wrong the first time, and the probe is fixed.** The first run of this
probe reported **242,810,880** against the file's own schema. **That was the offset of the last chunk, not
the first differing byte**: the probe's comparison returned the chunk boundary when the two files had
different lengths -- and they do, because the round trip's file is the shorter one. **The comparison now
finds the first byte that differs, and it compares the two files' data regions directly**, which is the
question a first differing byte cannot answer on its own. **The claim the wrong figure supported was
right** -- the difference is the metadata and not the data -- **and the figure was wrong**: the corrected
one is **242,857,739**, inside the metadata, and the data regions are identical. **That difference is
gone now**, because the metadata is the same too.

**What this cost.** **No public break, and no new type.** `FileSetSource::execute_rows` takes a
`&QueryPlan` and an `&ExecOptions` and gives back a `Dataset` -- all three already on the surface -- so the
surface list does not change and the crates can be published with this increment in them. **And the store
does not change at all**: `append_rows` takes the engine's rows and `Table::paths` gives the list of paths,
and the round trip is the embedder's three lines.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked
by the guard **inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/set-rows-run1.json`,
`bench-results/set-rows-run2.json` and `bench-results/set-rows-run3.json`, the reports this section
replaces are `bench-results/set-rows-spec044-before-run1.json` and its two siblings, and the probe is
`crates/columnar-store/examples/set_rows_probe.rs`.

---

### 3ab. The rows as they lie

**What this section is**: the rows a plan hands out, when they are **the rows that were read**. Spec 043
named the gather as the thing that stands between the engine and its own rows -- a copy of the whole table,
with the dictionary materialised away -- and **this is the increment that takes it away**, and measures what
is left.

**The rule.** Two things have to hold:

1. **No row was removed.** The filter's selection holds the survivors in the order they were read, and the
   selection's rows are distinct, so a selection as long as the dataset *is* every row -- whether the plan
   has no filter at all, or a filter that kept everything.
2. **Nothing reordered them.** A join's output and a sort's output are new rows, in a new order.

**And then the rows do not need to be copied at all.** The engine read the columns; it hands the same
columns out. **The lazy path moves them** -- the engine owns what it read -- **and the eager path copies
them**, because the caller owns the dataset there.

**One rule, both paths.** The test is about the **rows** and not about where they came from, so the eager
path and the lazy path make the same one. **They can answer it differently, because the read is
different**: the lazy path pushes the plan's predicates into the reader, so a predicate the reader took has
already removed its rows, and those rows come out as they lie. The eager path reads everything and filters
in the engine, so the survivors are scattered through the dataset, and they are gathered.

**The shape, and it is the plan's.** The rows come out with the reader's own encodings: on the published
file's schema, **7 dictionary columns** and **9 plain ones**. **So the caller can see which shape it got**
-- the encoding says so -- and **the store takes both**: its conversion maps the three dictionary forms and
the three plain ones ([ADR-0054](docs/adr/0054-the-engines-rows-reach-a-stores-table.md)).

**The cost: the gather is gone.**

| | the cost | before |
|---|---|---|
| the one file's rows out | **549.167 ms** | 3,338.468 ms |
| the eager rows: the copy of the columns | **154.858 ms** | 2,823.164 ms |
| the eager path's total | **706.634 ms** | 3,353.618 ms |

**The rows out are six times faster** -- **549.167 ms** against **3,338.468 ms** -- **and the copy that is
left is eighteen times smaller**: **154.858 ms** against **2,823.164 ms**. **The lazy path does not pay the
copy at all**: it moves the columns out of the reader, so the one file's rows cost the scan and nothing
else. **And the lazy path is now the cheaper of the two**, by 157.467 ms -- and that difference is the
copy.

**The round trip is the file, byte for byte.** [§3aa](#3aa-the-sets-rows) has the bytes: the round trip
under the file's own schema is **242,859,461** bytes and **no differing byte**, and **the store's own rule
still differs**, at byte **20,566,725**, because a column that holds no NULL is written REQUIRED. **That is
the store's rule and not the engine's**, and it is the one difference the round trip has left.

**And the estimate does not move**: **1,549,500,016 bytes**, for one file and for the set. **What the
budget is charged is what it was**; **what the plan actually uses went down**, and both are published.

**The gather does not go away.** It is the **right** thing to do when a filter removed rows in the engine, a
join or a sort made new rows: the survivors are scattered through the batch and have to be pulled out.
**The gather stays for every plan that needs it**, and §3z's plan's rows are gathered still -- their file
is **982,089** bytes, and it is a different file by construction.

**What it costs: nothing on the surface.** The rows are the same type they were -- a `Dataset` of column
chunks -- and **the store's half did not move at all**: its conversion already took both shapes, and its
writer, its grid, its commit and its schema rule are untouched. **No public break is taken**, so the crates
can be published with this increment in them.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked by
the guard **inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/set-rows-run1.json`,
`bench-results/set-rows-run2.json` and `bench-results/set-rows-run3.json`, and the probe is
`crates/columnar-store/examples/set_rows_probe.rs`.

---

### 3ac. The join-orders query, on both sides, taken apart

**What this section is**: the **6.0 ms** the engine is behind `DuckDB` by on the cold `join-orders`
query, given a place to live.

The engine's cold `join-orders` is **100.0 ms** against `DuckDB`'s **94.0 ms** — **6.0 ms** apart, while
the peer's is **175.3 ms** and `Polars`' **225.7 ms**. The set the bump replaces read **97.6 ms**,
**91.0 ms**, **168.8 ms** and **219.1 ms**, and the gap is **0.6 ms** smaller than it was, and the whole
of that is `DuckDB`'s own movement. **And every part of the engine's own analysis was set against
`DuckDB`'s _whole_**: [§3o](#3o-the-joins-stage-taken-apart) took the join's stage apart and found it
was the build side's read and `Arrow`'s decode of `orders.parquet`, and it was right — **about the
engine's own stage**.

**The rule.** **A part can be compared with a part. A total cannot be compared with a part.** So both
sides have to come apart, and each side's parts have to be that system's **own account of itself**.

**The instrument.** Four arms, **one session**, back to back, with the **order alternated** between the
iterations — a machine that drifts while an iteration runs gives the first measurement a different price
from the second. The iteration count is **even**, for the reason spec 026 gave.

| arm | what it is | what it yields |
|---|---|---|
| the engine | the engine's own plan over the file, the lazy path | the stage table, and the join's own parts |
| `DuckDB`, profiled | the same SQL, with `DuckDB`'s **own profiler** on | the operator tree's timings, the bytes read |
| `DuckDB`, unprofiled | the same SQL, the way the comparison harness runs it | the whole, with no parts |
| `DuckDB`, the filter off | the profiled arm with the **join filter pushdown disabled** | the parts, and the rows that reached the join |

**Both sides' answers are checked against the published value before anything is timed**, and the
profiler is not free, so the unprofiled arm is measured too: it is the whole the comparison harness
measures, and the difference from the profiled whole is the profiler's price.

#### The parts

| the part | the engine | `DuckDB` | `DuckDB`, the filter off |
|---|---|---|---|
| **the whole** | **96.241** ms | **92.735** ms | **138.154** ms |
| the probe side's read | 68.435 ms | 66.335 ms | 60.766 ms |
| the build side's read | 12.012 ms | 11.213 ms | 11.237 ms |
| the build side's open | 0.041 ms | *inside its scan* | *inside its scan* |
| **the join** | **14.625** ms | **7.706** ms | **59.149** ms |
| the projection | 0.533 ms | 0.081 ms | 0.087 ms |
| the aggregate | 0.275 ms | 0.433 ms | 0.543 ms |
| **outside the parts** | **0.019** ms | **6.879** ms | **6.597** ms |

The whole, unprofiled — the way the comparison harness measures it — is **92.000** ms, against the
profiled **92.735** ms: the profiler costs about **0.7 ms**.

**And the engine's scan does not carry the probe's cost.** On the cold path the probe runs **inside**
the scan and its time is taken out of the scan's, so the scan's figure and the join's figure are two
different parts of the query. **And `DuckDB`'s whole carries 6.879 ms outside its operator tree** — its
planning, its blocked threads and its I/O — where the engine's plan output names every stage and
leaves **0.019** outside.

#### The paired differences

Both sides are measured back to back in the same iteration, so the machine's drift cannot land on one
side. **The difference is the paired one**, not the difference of two medians.

| the difference, in the same iteration | the median | the least | the most |
|---|---|---|---|
| **the whole: the engine less `DuckDB`** | **+3.290** ms | −2.350 ms | +16.606 ms |
| the probe side's read: the engine less `DuckDB` | +2.111 ms | −1.006 ms | +11.016 ms |
| the build side's read: the engine less `DuckDB` | +0.862 ms | +0.060 ms | +1.316 ms |
| **the join: the engine less `DuckDB`** | **+6.898** ms | +3.385 ms | +10.963 ms |
| the projection: the engine less `DuckDB` | +0.452 ms | +0.434 ms | +0.527 ms |
| the aggregate: the engine less `DuckDB` | −0.165 ms | −0.232 ms | −0.081 ms |
| outside the parts: the engine less `DuckDB` | −6.860 ms | −7.876 ms | −6.505 ms |

**The sign is what carries the claim.** The engine's join is slower in **every one of the eighteen
iterations**, by **+6.898 ms** at the median and by at least **+3.385 ms** in the quietest. The engine's
build side's read is slower in every iteration; its projection is slower in every iteration; its
aggregate is **faster** in every iteration. **The probe side's read is at the noise** — the engine's is
ahead in **three** of the eighteen iterations and behind in fifteen — and **the whole is at the noise**
too, for the reason the next paragraph gives.

#### The rows, and the counterfactual

**How many rows each side's join saw**:

| | rows that reached the join | rows that came out |
|---|---|---|
| the engine | **6,000,000** | 216,783 |
| `DuckDB` | **216,783** | 216,783 |
| `DuckDB`, the filter off | **5,999,998** | 216,783 |

**And the counterfactual changes exactly one thing.** With its join filter pushdown disabled, `DuckDB`'s
own profile no longer carries the filter at all — `(l_orderkey >= 6)`, the statistics-derived minimum, is
still there, and `optional: l_orderkey IN PRF(o_orderkey)`, the perfect-hash filter over the build
side's keys, is **gone**:

| the counterfactual | the median | the least | the most |
|---|---|---|---|
| **the join: `DuckDB` with the filter off less `DuckDB`** | **+51.422** ms | +48.013 ms | +56.615 ms |
| **the join: `DuckDB` with the filter off less the engine** | **+44.190** ms | +38.000 ms | +48.548 ms |
| the probe side's read: `DuckDB` with the filter off less `DuckDB` | −5.827 ms | −9.251 ms | −2.809 ms |
| the whole: `DuckDB` with the filter off less `DuckDB` | +45.697 ms | +38.080 ms | +53.889 ms |

**So `DuckDB`'s own join, over the same six million rows, costs 44.190 ms more than the engine's.** The
engine's lookup is the **faster** of the two — by a factor of four — and the engine is behind because it
**looks up every row**.

#### The finding

**The gap is not the lookup. The gap is that there is no lookup to do.**

1. **The engine's join is the slower one — 14.625 ms against 7.706 ms — and it is slower for a reason
   that is not the engine's lookup.** `DuckDB` joins **216,783** rows; the engine joins **6,000,000**.
2. **And the engine's lookup is the better one.** `DuckDB`'s hash join, over the same six million rows,
   costs **+51.422 ms** more than its own filtered join and **+44.190 ms** more than the engine's. **The
   engine's probe is four times cheaper than `DuckDB`'s hash join, row for row.**
3. **The filter is what `DuckDB` has and the engine does not**, and it is worth **+45.697 ms** on the
   whole: it costs the scan **5.827 ms** and saves the join **51.422 ms**.
4. **And the rest of the engine's disadvantage is in the reads**: the build side's read is **+0.862 ms**
   and the projection **+0.452 ms**, against a probe side's read that is at the noise.
5. **And `DuckDB`'s planning gives back most of it**: **6.860 ms** outside its operator tree, where the
   engine's whole leaves **0.019** outside its stages.

**The arithmetic closes.** The engine's parts are **+10.668 ms** more than `DuckDB`'s; `DuckDB`'s whole
carries **6.860 ms** outside its operator tree that the engine's does not; and the net is the whole's
**+3.290 ms**. **The decomposition closes to 0.0000 ms on every one of the eighteen iterations.**

**And the whole is at the noise on this host.** The published gap is **6.0 ms**; this host's paired gap
is **+3.290 ms**, and in one of the eighteen iterations the engine's whole was **ahead**. **The published
figures stand** — they are the comparison harness's, taken by a different campaign — and **this section
does not re-derive them**. What this section publishes is the **parts**, and they are the same on both
sides whichever way the whole falls.

#### The bytes

`DuckDB` read **79,321,951** bytes in sixteen of the eighteen iterations, and **88,463,611** in two; with
the filter off, **79,321,951** in seventeen and **97,606,309** in one. **The engine's bytes are not
measured here** — its read is named by the **columns** it reports, `l_orderkey` and `l_extendedprice`,
which are `DuckDB`'s columns too — and the file's own column sizes are the file's, not a measurement of
the engine's read. **The two sides read the same columns**, and the two sides' answers are the same
answer.

#### What this changes

**The next question is not "why is the engine behind". It is "what does a filter over the build side's
keys cost, and what does it save".**

The build side's keys are known **before** the probe side's scan runs, so they could be a filter on it:
a bit test per row instead of a hash lookup per row. `DuckDB`'s own filter costs its scan **5.827 ms**
over six million rows and saves its join **51.422 ms**. **The engine's prize, if its filter costs what
`DuckDB`'s costs, is the whole of its own join — 14.625 ms — less what the filter would cost it.** That
is the next increment, and **this section does not build it**: the measurement decides what is built, and
the decision belongs to the increment that follows.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked by
the guard **inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/join-parts-run1.json`,
`bench-results/join-parts-run2.json` and `bench-results/join-parts-run3.json`, and the probe is
`crates/columnar-bench/src/join_parts.rs`, driven by `just probe-join-parts`. The decision is in
[ADR-0058](docs/adr/0058-the-join-orders-query-on-both-sides-taken-apart.md).

**The conditions**: three campaigns of six iterations, on one host, with the load average between
**8.54** and **8.77** — and the host's own drift is published with every difference, because a
difference of two medians would let the drift land on both sides.


### 3ad. The build side's keys as a filter

**What this section is**: what a filter over the build side's keys costs, and what it saves — the question
[§3ac](#3ac-the-join-orders-query-on-both-sides-taken-apart) named and did not answer.

**The question was this.** The engine's join is **+6.898 ms** behind `DuckDB`'s, and `DuckDB` joins
**216,783** rows where the engine joins **6,000,000**, because `DuckDB` pushes the build side's keys into
the probe side's scan as a filter and the engine has no such filter. `DuckDB`'s own filter costs its scan
**5.827 ms** over six million rows and saves its join **51.422 ms**. **The engine's prize, if its filter
costs what `DuckDB`'s costs, is the whole of its own join — 14.625 ms — less what the filter would cost
it.**

**This section does not build the filter. It measures what the filter costs, and the measurement decides
what is built.** A filter is not built because it is a good idea; it is built because it pays.

#### The instrument

`crates/columnar-engine/examples/key_filter_probe.rs`, driven by `just probe-key-filter`. It is the house's
ladder, and every rung is measured **in the iteration**:

| the rung | what it is |
|---|---|
| **the table's own construction** | the table's own bytes, and the build side's keys over them |
| **the filter's own construction** | the filter's own bits, and the build side's keys over them |
| **the table's own load** | the byte, one a probe row: the lookup with nothing else in it |
| **the filter's own load** | the bit, one a probe row: the test with nothing else in it |
| **the probe as it is** | the engine's own probe, line for line, without the filter |
| **the filter in front of the table** | the engine's own probe, line for line, with the filter in the lookup |
| **the filter compacting the rows** | the filter's test over every row, and the survivors alone probed |
| **the probe over the survivors alone** | the join the engine would be left with if the rows were kept out of it |
| **the engine's own probe** | the control: the engine's own probe, over the same column, beside them |

**And the control is what makes the rest of the table a statement about the engine.** The candidates are
the engine's `probe_direct`, **line for line**, and the engine's own probe is measured in the same
iteration, over the same column: **7.622 ms** against the candidate's **7.967 ms**. A rung that is not the
engine's probe would price a shape the engine does not have.

**The probe side's column is materialised afresh in every iteration**, and it is not copied. The engine's
own query materialises the probe column and then probes it, so the column it probes is one the reader has
**just written**; a column held warm across the iterations would be a column the engine does not pay for.
**And that is the whole of the filter's case.** The table is **5,999,995** bytes and the filter
**750,000**; the filter only wins if the probe column's stream evicts the table and leaves the filter. A
column kept warm would not evict anything, and the filter would be measured on a shape that does not
exist.

**And the order alternates.** The six orders are chosen so that **every pair** of candidates is measured
back to back in **four of the six** iterations, **two in each direction** — a rotation of three would have
put one pair back to back four times and another twice.

#### The parts

| the rung | the median | the least | the most |
|---|---|---|---|
| the table's own construction | 0.672 ms | 0.620 ms | 1.310 ms |
| the filter's own construction | 0.201 ms | 0.179 ms | 0.307 ms |
| **the table's own load** | **4.399** ms | 4.049 ms | 5.308 ms |
| **the filter's own load** | **5.222** ms | 5.082 ms | 5.788 ms |
| **the probe as it is** | **7.967** ms | 7.005 ms | 16.887 ms |
| the filter in front of the table | 10.770 ms | 9.085 ms | 21.516 ms |
| the filter compacting the rows | 8.141 ms | 7.837 ms | 8.799 ms |
| the probe over the survivors alone | 0.906 ms | 0.858 ms | 1.227 ms |
| the engine's own probe (the control) | 7.622 ms | 7.103 ms | 9.369 ms |

#### The paired differences

The two sides of every difference are measured **back to back in the same iteration**, so the machine's
drift cannot land on one side. **The difference is the paired one**, not the difference of two medians.

| the difference, in the same iteration | the median | the least | the most |
|---|---|---|---|
| **the filter in front of the table, less the probe as it is** | **+2.318** ms | −3.925 ms | +12.974 ms |
| **the filter compacting the rows, less the probe as it is** | **+0.547** ms | −8.444 ms | +1.188 ms |
| **the prize: the probe as it is, less the probe over the survivors** | **+6.981** ms | +6.110 ms | +16.029 ms |

**The sign is what carries the claim, and the filter in front of the table has one.** Ten of its twelve
paired samples are positive, and the two that are not are the two iterations this host stalled in: in one
of them the stall landed on the filter's own measurement and in the other on the probe's, and the
difference moved with it. **The filter's own load is more than the table's own load — 5.222 ms against
4.399 ms** — and that is the whole of the claim.

**And the shape that replaces the whole probe is a wash.** The filter compacting the rows costs
**+0.547 ms** at the median, and **four of its twelve paired samples are negative**: the sign is not
established, and a wash is not a win.

#### The finding

**The prize is real. The prize is unreachable. And the reason is that the engine's lookup is already the
cheapest membership test there is.**

1. **The prize is worth +6.981 ms.** The probe over the survivors alone costs **0.906 ms**, against the
   probe as it is at **7.967 ms**: if the rows that cannot match never reached the probe, the engine's
   join would be **6.981 ms** smaller. **That is the prize §3ac named**, and it is measured, not assumed.
2. **And a filter's test costs more than the lookup it would replace.** The filter's own load over the
   same six million rows is **5.222 ms**; the table's own load — one random access into the table's own
   bytes — is **4.399 ms**. **Both are one random access, and the bitmap's test carries a shift and a
   mask on top of the access the byte does not.** The direct-addressed table's byte **is** the membership
   test: the key's own range addresses it, and it consults nothing. **A filter can only be consulted in
   addition to it, and what it is consulted in addition to is cheaper than it is.**
3. **And the probe is not much more than the test.** The probe as it is costs **7.967 ms** over six
   million rows, and the whole of it would have to go for the filter to pay: the filter's own test is
   **5.222 ms** of that, before its construction (**0.201 ms**) and before the branch that skips the
   table's byte. **The shape that replaces the whole probe — the filter compacting the rows, so that the
   survivors alone are probed — is a wash at +0.547 ms.**
4. **So the filter is not built.** Not in front of the table, and not compacting the rows. The engine
   keeps no filter, the memory estimate does not move, and the increment changed **no engine work at all**.

**And what survives the refusal is the mechanism, and it is a statement about `DuckDB` too.**
`DuckDB`'s filter costs its scan **5.827 ms** over six million rows — the same order as this filter's
**5.222 ms** — and it saves its join **51.422 ms**. **The same test, in the same place, is worth two
orders of magnitude more.** The difference is not the test: it is what the test replaces. `DuckDB`'s
filter replaces a **hash join**, which costs **+44.190 ms** more than the engine's lookup over the same
six million rows; a filter here would replace **a lookup that is already a single load with no hash**.
**`DuckDB`'s filter is the price of `DuckDB`'s join, and the engine does not have that join to pay for.**

#### What this changes

**The next question is not "how can a filter be made cheaper". It is "where does the engine's join spend
the rest of its time".**

The engine's join part is **14.625 ms**; its probe over six million rows is **7.967 ms** and its table's
construction is **0.672 ms**, and the filter is not where the rest of it is — a filter cannot make the
lookup cheaper, because the lookup **is** the membership test. **And the engine's advantage over
`DuckDB` is already taken: `DuckDB`'s join is four times dearer a row, and that is why its filter is
worth having.**

So the next increment has to find the join's other work — the part the probe does not account for — and
ask what it is. **That is the next question, and this section does not answer it.**

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked by
the guard **inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/key-filter-run1.json`,
`bench-results/key-filter-run2.json` and `bench-results/key-filter-run3.json`, and the probe is
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/key_filter_probe.rs`, driven by `just probe-key-filter`. The decision is
in [ADR-0059](docs/adr/0059-the-build-side-s-keys-as-a-filter.md).

**The conditions**: three campaigns of six iterations, on one host, with the load average between **5.85**
and **6.37** — and the host's own drift is published with every difference, because a difference of two
medians would let the drift land on both sides. **Three of the eighteen iterations carried a host stall
of about eight milliseconds**, and they are in the spreads above: one landed on the probe as it is, one on
the filter's own measurement, and one on the probe's again. **The medians are what the house publishes,
and the medians do not move for a stall in three of eighteen.**


---

## 4. The defects the increments found

Twenty-nine, all found by measurement, all recorded with their before-and-after numbers in the ADRs
and in the commit history.

| | before | after | where |
|---|---|---|---|
| The kernel's mask accumulation | 4.81 G rows/s (0.84× the compiler) | 8.84 G rows/s (1.51×) | ADR-0006 |
| The global aggregate: a key vector and a hash lookup per row, for one group | `scan` 48.9 ms | `scan` 0.0 ms | `columnar-engine/src/operators/aggregate.rs` |
| The dictionary map: a mispredicted branch per row | `q6` filter 41.2 ms | `q6` filter 6.7 ms | `columnar-engine/src/operators/filter.rs` |
| The reader decoded each predicate column **twice**, once per predicate | `q6` cold 358 ms | `q6` cold 168 ms | `columnar-engine/src/lib.rs` |
| The Arrow-to-chunk conversion copied the dictionary's distinct values **per batch** — 1.8 million string allocations on the lazy path, 14.6 million on the eager one | `q6` cold 137 ms (lazy) · 1890 ms (eager) | `q6` cold 121 ms (lazy) · 1181 ms (eager) | `columnar-engine/src/dataset.rs` |
| The pushdown's mask went through Arrow's `BooleanBuilder`, whose per-element capacity check cost more than decoding the columns did | `q6` cold 137 ms | `q6` cold 126 ms | `columnar-engine/src/pushdown.rs` |
| The pushdown compared values **one row at a time**, in Rust, while the filter ran the SIMD kernel — 6.4× slower than the engine's own filter on the same four predicates over the same six million rows | `q6` pushdown 42.9 ms | `q6` pushdown 6.1 ms | `columnar-engine/src/pushdown.rs` |
| **The `--threads` flag never reached the operators.** The resident path passed `ExecOptions::default()`, whose `threads` is `None`, and `None` means one thread — so the operators could not have been parallel through it, however much the code had been parallelised | no scaling at all: 13.8 ms at one worker and 13.8 ms at six | `q6` filter: 6.9 ms at one worker, **2.0 ms** at six | `columnar-cli/src/main.rs` |
| The serial filter **copied** the chunk's validity mask where it used to borrow it, because the parallel path needed a block's own copy | one copy of the whole mask per predicate | a borrow | `columnar-engine/src/operators/filter.rs` |
| **The eager path used the Parquet reader's default batch size** — 1,024 rows — while the lazy path used 8,192. The per-batch cost scales with the number of columns, and the eager path reads all sixteen, so it rebuilt sixteen dictionary tables 5,860 times instead of 733 | eager load 1145.6 ms | eager load **587.0 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/dataset.rs` |
| The merge was a **fold**, so it copied the accumulated result once per part and grew with the square of the number of parts. For the validity masks that was an allocation storm — a fresh 750 KB mask at every step | 6-thread eager load 366.7 ms | 6-thread eager load **276.4 ms** | `columnar-core/src/null.rs` |
| The merge **repointed the NULL rows' dictionary indices** at real values. A NULL row's index is undefined — the convention is slot zero, and the validity mask says the row is NULL — but the merge treated the placeholder as a live reference. No answer changed; the chunk's bytes did | the parallel chunk's bytes differed from the serial chunk's | identical | `columnar-engine/src/dataset.rs` |
| **The pushdown's mask was handed to the reader as a row filter, and a row filter is not a mask.** parquet-rs turned it into a list of **2,775,138 row ranges** for 2,178,861 surviving rows, and applied the list one range at a time | reading `l_shipdate` through the list: **50.0 ms** · `q6` cold **82.4 ms** | reading it without the list: **7.8 ms** · `q6` cold **26.2 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/pushdown.rs` |
| **The projection materialised a column that nobody would ever see.** A global aggregate gathered the survivors into a column of their own and then summed it — and a plan with an aggregation ignores the projection, because the aggregate result is the output | `filter-range` resident **14.6 ms** · project stage **7.8 ms** | `filter-range` resident **6.9 ms** · project stage **0.0 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/aggregate.rs` |
| **The scan filtered the whole batch and copied the survivors twice.** Arrow's `filter_record_batch` copied **every** column of the batch — including the predicate's column, which the query does not materialise and which was about to be thrown away — and the survivors were then copied *again* out of the filtered batch into the engine's column | the filter and the copy **4.7 ms** | the compaction **2.5 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/dataset.rs` |
| **The lazy path's batch size was inherited, not measured.** The 8,192 rows came from a measurement of a *different* read — the eager path's — and the lazy path reads one to three columns, not sixteen | lazy scan at 8,192 **18.6 ms** | lazy scan at 65,536 **17.4 ms** — and spec 016 changed it again; see the last row | `columnar-engine/src/source.rs` |
| **The reader's batch size divided no row group's row count.** The engine's batch was 65,536 rows, and the files' row groups hold 1,000,000, 500,000 and 250,000 — so every row group ended on a **partial** batch, and a partial batch costs **0.78 ms**. The engine paid a per-row-group cost `DuckDB`, `Polars` and the `DataFusion` peer did not pay, and the file with the most parallelism was the slowest | the engine's one-thread Q6 rose **19.7 → 34.6 ms** across the three files — **0.83 ms a row group** | **15.4 / 15.7 / 16.4 ms** — flat | `columnar-engine/src/source.rs` |
| **The join's probe hashed with `SipHash`.** The default hasher is built to be resistant to collision attacks — a property a query engine's hash table has no use for, and a cost it does: the probe is the join's hot loop, and it runs once for every row of the probe side. The first implementation ran at about 36 ns a row | the join stage **219.9 ms** · the whole lazy query **318.9 ms** | the join stage **117.7 ms** · the whole lazy query **199.5 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/join.rs` |
| **The join's table was a `HashMap` over a 32-byte enum.** The key's discriminant was hashed with the payload, the key was stored whether it was a number or a string, and every probe was a random access into a table of a few megabytes. The build side's keys are integers in a dense range: a table that is **one byte a key**, indexed by the key itself, makes a probe a single load — no hash, no chain | the engine's probe **102.7 ms** · the join stage **128.1 ms** | the engine's probe **9.0 ms** · the join stage **21.9 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/join.rs` |
| **The key reader asked the validity bitmap about every row**, whether or not the column held a single NULL. A bitmap of a column with no NULLs says nothing, and it was read once a row, in the join's probe and in the sort | reading the key column **9.3 ms** | reading the column's own integers **1.7 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/key.rs` |
| **The sort's bounded heap kept the best row instead of the worst.** The heap's ordering was reversed, so the row it dropped was the one it should have kept. The query still returned *a* sum — a wrong one | `sort-topk` answered **500025.13** | `sort-topk` answers **1049999.60** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/sort.rs` |
