| **The sort built a 24-byte `KeyValue` enum for every row**, because one of the enum's variants is a string — and read the key through the validity bitmap on the way | the engine's bounded sort **13.3 ms** | the engine's bounded sort **3.7 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/sort.rs` |
| **The executor kept two numberings.** The survivors' positions and the dataset's row numbers, and a translation between them — a 24 MB vector of `0..n` allocated, filled and thrown away on every join query | the identity `positions` vector: **24 MB** | gone | `columnar-engine/src/lib.rs` |
| **The join's build side was charged the file's whole row count** for a table bounded by its distinct keys, and under-charged its own worst case by 2.4×. **A global aggregate was charged sixteen bytes a row** for a plan with one group — 91.6 MiB of phantom, on every eager plan. **The row-position vectors were counted nowhere** | the join's build side charged **1,500,000** rows | bounded by the file's own row-group statistics | `columnar-engine/src/memory.rs` |
| **The plan output said `project 6000000 6000000`** for a projection that gathered 216,783 rows | the project stage: **6,000,000** | the project stage: **216,783** | `columnar-engine/src/lib.rs` |
| **The probe's own direct-addressed table answered for a key below its base.** The index wrapped round to zero, so a key smaller than the smallest build key was answered with the base key's count. The instrument had a defect, and its own row-for-row check found it | the probe's direct table: **2 extra rows** on 216,783 | the probe's direct table: **0 extra rows** | `crates/columnar-engine/examples/operator_probe.rs` |
| **The plan output left time outside every stage.** The join and the sort asked the filter's selection for its **row numbers** before either stage's clock started, so a plan with no filter built **six million** row numbers — **24 MB** — and the stage table did not account for it | the plan output's hole: **4.2 ms** of the join's query and **4.2 ms** of the sort's | the plan output's hole: **0.016 ms** — the account closes | `crates/columnar-engine/src/lib.rs` |
| The join's lookup consulted the **overflow map** once a probe row, for a case that does not happen: the map holds **nothing** for this data, because every key's count fits in its byte, and the lookup paid for it on every one of six million rows. The map's share was measured for the first time in spec 028, and it is **at or below the noise** | the map's share: **0.183 ms** of a **4.417 ms** remainder | no map to consult — **the map is not allocated at all** when nothing overflows | `columnar-engine/src/operators/join.rs` |
| **The instrument measured two things that are not differences of the code.** The engine's lookup is a **private** method and the instrument is another crate, so across the boundary it was not inlined and the instrument paid a **call a probe row** that the engine's own query does not pay; and the candidate read a **warm** buffer while the engine's probe read one the reader had **just written**. Both were inside the remainder spec 028 published as the engine's cost | the remainder: **3.337 ms**, of which the crate boundary is **+1.332 ms** and the buffer **1.158 ms** | the remainder: **0.109 ms** — the engine's probe **8.395 ms** against the hand-written candidate's **8.315 ms** | `crates/columnar-engine/examples/join_cost_probe.rs` |
| **The sort compared every key a row at a time, in Rust.** The comparison — one per key, six million of them — was a scalar loop, and it was **2.2 ms** of the operator's **3.2 ms**: the read was never the cost, the counting was. The same column, compared in blocks against the heap's own worst key, costs **0.96 ms** | the engine's bounded sort **3.23 ms** · the sort's stage **4.18 ms** | the engine's bounded sort **1.02 ms** · the sort's stage **1.10 ms** | `columnar-engine/src/operators/sort.rs` |

The global aggregate is the one worth calling out from the first increment: the module
documentation promised that `COUNT(*)` was an in-place fast path that reads no column, and it did
read no column — but it still allocated a key vector and did a hash-map lookup for every one of
the six million rows, in order to arrive at the same answer every time. The fix is a separate
path for the global case, where there is exactly one group and there never was a key.

The three rows about the reader, the conversion and the mask are from spec 002's increment, and
they are the same kind of defect: work done per batch, per predicate or per dictionary, where the
shape of the problem does not need it. The reader decodes a column once per projection mask that
mentions it, so two predicates on one column means two decodes — they are now pushed as one. The
conversion was copying the dictionary's distinct values out of every batch to merge them, when the
merge is a lookup and needs no copy. And the mask was being built through a builder whose
per-element capacity check costs more than the comparison it was carrying, when the mask is a bit
vector and can be written a word at a time.

The figure for that one, on the published median of three runs, was **84.4 ms** in the set that
followed the pushdown rewrite and **82.4 ms** in the set after it; it is **15.3 ms** in the set this one
replaces and **14.8 ms** in this one, because the machine is no longer under memory
pressure.

One of the three turned out to be worth more than the increment that found it. The dictionary copy
was invisible on the path spec 002 was building, because the lazy path never materialises the
column — and it was worth 1.60× to the path it was not building, because the eager path
materialises all sixteen columns across 5,860 batches. See [§2b-bis](#2b-bis-the-eager-path-got-faster-too-and-it-is-measured).

The pushdown row is the same kind of defect, found the same way: **a total that was never
attributed**. The scan's 121.5 ms was never split into the reader's decode and the comparison,
and the comparison turned out to be 42.9 ms of it — the larger half of the single-column scans,
and 6.4× slower than the engine's own filter on the same work. A total is not an explanation,
and the plan output now says which half is which.

**Spec 010's row is the same kind of defect again: work done for a shape the
problem does not have.** The projection gathered 2,178,861 survivors into a column of their own —
17.4 MB of values — and the aggregate summed that column and threw it away, because a plan with
an aggregation ignores the projection. The global aggregate has one group and never needs its
values aligned with anything, so the copy was never needed. It cost **7.8 ms of a 14.6 ms query**,
and it is the whole of the one query the engine used to lose. See
[ADR-0016](docs/adr/0016-the-aggregate-reads-in-place.md).

**The last three rows are spec 008's, and they are the same kind of mistake again** — this time
about *constants* and about *how work is combined* rather than about work done per batch. The
batch size was inherited rather than measured. The merge grew with the square of the number of
parts. And the merge treated a NULL row's placeholder index as a live reference. The first was
worth 1.95× on its own, the second 90 ms at six parts, and the third changed no answer but did
change the bytes. See [ADR-0014](docs/adr/0014-parallel-eager-decode.md).

**And the row before it is spec 016's, and it is the same kind of mistake for the third time: a constant
where the file's shape belonged.** Spec 008 measured a batch size and found one that was too
small; spec 016 found that the *right* answer was not a number at all but the file's own row-group
divisor — 65,536 was a constant that happened to divide no row group in any of the three files. The
cost was invisible in the shape the tree measured in, because that file's row group happened to be
friendly, and it was worth **0.83 ms a row group** on the files that were not. The engine's own
one-thread Q6 is now flat across the three files, where it was 76% apart. See
[ADR-0023](docs/adr/0023-the-per-row-group-cost.md).

**And the row after it is spec 020's, and it is the same kind of mistake for the fourth time: a
default where the job's own hasher belonged.** `std`'s default hasher is `SipHash`, which is built
to be resistant to collision attacks — a property a query engine's hash table has no use for, and a
cost it does, because the probe is the join's hot loop and it runs once for every row of the probe
side. The first implementation ran at about 36 ns a row; with a hash written for the job the join
stage went from **219.9 ms** to **117.7 ms** and the whole lazy query from **318.9 ms** to
**199.5 ms**, on the same file, in the same session, with the same answer. See
[ADR-0027](docs/adr/0027-the-engine-joins-and-sorts.md).

**And the eight rows after it are spec 021's, and they are the same kind of mistake for the fifth
time: a general shape where the job's own shape belonged.** The table was a `HashMap` over an enum
where an integer key wanted an array; the key reader consulted a bitmap that carried no
information; the sort built an enum per row where a number would do; the executor carried two
numberings where one would do; the estimates charged a file's row count where the file's
statistics would do. The measurement is in
[§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart), and the code's share of the published tables is measured
against the old code on the same host. See
[ADR-0028](docs/adr/0028-the-hot-paths-taken-apart.md).

**And one of those eight rows is a defect the increment *introduced*, and the instrument caught it.**
The sort's new fast path reversed the bounded heap's ordering, so the heap kept the best row and
dropped the worst — and the query still returned a sum, just the wrong one: **500025.13** instead of
**1049999.60**. Two things caught it: the differential tests, which compare the sort against a
reference sort row for row, and the probe's own check, which compares every candidate against the
engine's output before it times anything. The second is the one worth keeping: **an instrument that
does not check its own answers measures a different operator than the one it claims to.**

---

## 5. Not measured

| | why | status |
|---|---|---|
| **x86-64 / AVX2 kernel numbers** | There is no x86-64 machine **on the author's desk**. There is one in GitHub's hosted fleet, and the ratios are measured there. | The AVX2 path's **correctness** is verified twice: under Rosetta 2 on this machine, with the row counter proving the instructions ran, and on **native x86-64 hardware**, where the differential suite passes and the mask matches the oracle's bit for bit. Its **performance** is published as **ratios**, per **processor**, because the hosted runners are a fleet and the ratio is a property of the processor as much as of the kernel: **three processors are published**, with 3 to 11 campaigns each, and **three more ran the campaign once each and are not published**. The **absolute** figures are not published: they describe a shared runner, and this repository does not publish a number it cannot reproduce. See [§1b](#1b-the-kernel-on-x86-64-measured-natively). |
| **The blueprint's "AVX2 6.7× scalar"** | It was measured on an EPYC / DDR5 / NVMe machine that is not this one. | Not reproduced, not quoted as this project's own. |
| **Page-level pruning** | The `ArrowPredicate` path evaluates over the decoded batch, so the reader fetches every page of the predicate's column — but so does DuckDB, because **this file has no page index**: the generator writes `EnabledStatistics::Chunk`. DuckDB's own plan says `Row Groups Scanned: 6 / 6`. | **Not the gap.** It was published as the gap once and was wrong. Page pruning would be a *further* win on top, and it needs a `RowSelection` computed from a page index that does not exist in this file, or the row-format predicate — which computes a different function. See [ADR-0010](docs/adr/0010-pushdown-equivalence.md) and [§2c](#2c-the-lazy-scan-and-what-it-reads). |
| **A vectorised arithmetic kernel** | The expression evaluator is a scalar loop over the rows that survived the filter. | **Not implemented, and the measurement says it does not matter yet**: the `evaluate` stage is **0.2 ms** of the real Q6's 70 ms — 0.3%. The query's cost is reading `l_extendedprice`, not computing with it. The arithmetic is evaluated over the **survivors**, not over the file, which is the design decision that makes it cheap. See [§3c](#3c-the-real-q6s-stages-and-what-the-arithmetic-costs) and [ADR-0012](docs/adr/0012-arithmetic-expression.md). |
| **Parallel decode** | **Measured — spec 005.** The read splits the file's row groups across the workers and preserves the file's row order. The engine gets **3.89×** from six workers, against DuckDB's **3.30×**, on the identical query and file. | **The gap was 2.1× at one thread and is 2.5× at six.** The reader-speed term has since changed sign — the engine's full-file decode is now **2.3× faster** than DuckDB's — and the architecture term is now mostly *how much of the query is scan*: the engine parallelises nearly all of its work, DuckDB parallelises its scan and its filter over the survivors. See [§2e](#2e-the-thread-count-sweep) and [ADR-0011](docs/adr/0011-parallel-decode.md). |
| **The parallelism above the file's row-group count** | The read splits *row groups*, and this file has **six**. Six workers is the ceiling, whatever the machine has — for the lazy read, the operators *and* the eager decode alike. | **Measured — spec 015, and the defect it found was fixed by spec 016.** The row-group size is now a knob, and files with **twelve** and **twenty-four** row groups were swept and probed beside the six-group one, in one session. The worker count is `min(threads, row_groups)` at every point of every sweep. The cap is the file's, not the engine's — **and it is not free**: the engine paid about **0.83 ms a row group** that DuckDB, Polars and the Rust peer did not pay, so the file with the most parallelism was the **slowest** in absolute terms. **Spec 016 measured the mechanism and removed it**: the reader's batch size is derived from the file's row groups now, the engine's one-thread figure is flat across the three files (15.4 / 15.7 / 16.4 ms), and the speedup at fourteen threads is **3.74×**, **3.63×** and **3.39×** — set by the parallelism alone, not by a serial cost that grew with the file. See [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs), [ADR-0022](docs/adr/0022-the-parallelism-above-the-row-group-count.md) and [ADR-0023](docs/adr/0023-the-per-row-group-cost.md). |
| **The parallel operators** | **Measured — spec 007.** The filter and the projection split their work across the workers, and the answer does not depend on the thread count. The **aggregate** is not parallelised, and never will be: `SUM` is not associative. | **The engine's resident query gets 4.27× from fourteen workers** on `q6`, against DuckDB's 5.75× — and the engine is ahead at every thread count measured: **5.9× faster at one thread, 4.4× faster at fourteen**. The operators' worker count is not capped by the file's row groups, so it goes to fourteen where the read's stops at six. See [§2f](#2f-the-resident-sweep-where-the-operators-are-the-measurement) and [ADR-0013](docs/adr/0013-parallel-operators.md). |
| **The eager path's parallel decode** | **Measured — spec 008.** The eager path splits the file's row groups across the workers and merges the parts in block order, and the merged dataset is bit-identical to the serial one's. | **The load went from 1277.8 ms to 550.2 ms** — 2.32× — and the engine went from **behind** DuckDB to **2.2× ahead** of it at one thread in this set (2.1× in the set before it). Most of the win was a batch size the eager path had inherited rather than measured; the rest was the parallel decode. **Spec 016 gave this path the same derived batch rule the lazy path got**, and the load sweep is the only published measurement of that change. See [§2g](#2g-the-load-sweep--where-the-engines-biggest-number-was) and [ADR-0014](docs/adr/0014-parallel-eager-decode.md). |
| **The lazy scan's decomposition** | The plan output reports the scan stage and the pushdown's cost inside it — 15.4 ms and 7.3 ms of it — but **not** how much of the scan is Arrow's own decode and how much is the engine's own work. | **Measured — spec 009, and again in spec 011.** The decomposition is in the tree, as `crates/columnar-engine/examples/lazy_scan_decomposition.rs` and `crates/columnar-engine/examples/filter_range_scan_decomposition.rs`: Arrow's own decode of the two columns is **10.7 ms**, the predicates **0.7–1.4 ms**, and the engine's own work on top of the decode the rest. Splitting the scan the way the eager decode was split is what found the range list, and the range list was 76% of the scan; splitting it again is what found the double copy. See [ADR-0015](docs/adr/0015-the-engine-keeps-the-mask.md) and [ADR-0017](docs/adr/0017-the-scan-filters-only-what-it-keeps.md). |
| **The official TPC-H SF-1 file** | The generator produces a TPC-H-*shaped* file with approximated distributions, UTF-8 dates and a seeded PRNG. | Said in as many words in the manifest and in [constitution principle VI](constitution.md). |
| **`SUM` over a computed expression** | **Measured — spec 006.** The arithmetic aggregate evaluates `l_extendedprice * (1 - l_discount)` over the rows that survived the filter and sums the result. | **Measured, and it is cheap.** The `evaluate` stage is **0.2 ms** of the real Q6's 70 ms — 0.3% — because the arithmetic runs over the **survivors** and not over the file. The comparison publishes Q6 itself as `q6-revenue`, beside Q6's *predicate* set. See [§3c](#3c-the-real-q6s-stages-and-what-the-arithmetic-costs) and [ADR-0012](docs/adr/0012-arithmetic-expression.md). |
| **Window functions, decimal, dates, nested types, GPU** | Out of scope for spec 001. | The **hash join and the sort** were in this list and are built now — spec 020 — and the two queries that use them are published in [§2](#2-the-engine-against-duckdb-polars-and-datafusion) with the rest. The others are listed in the spec's out-of-scope table. |

---

## 5a. The performance gate

The constitution requires two gates — no statistically significant regression against a stored
baseline, and absolute budgets met — and until spec 004 neither existed. They do now, and they
are worth describing because of what they found.

`just perf-gate` measures the kernel, 32 independent blocks of 5 timed passes each, and compares
the samples against `bench-results/kernel-baseline.json` with a **Mann-Whitney U test**: the
exact permutation distribution for small samples, the normal approximation with tie and
continuity corrections for large ones. It is one-sided, in the direction "the current sample is
slower".

**The first version was statistically correct and useless.** Five consecutive runs against an
unchanged baseline produced:

| flavor | runs reporting a regression | the difference |
|---|---|---|
| `cpp-auto` | 5 of 5 | 1.3% – 2.3% slower |
| `cpp-simd` | 4 of 5 | 1.7% – 3.9% slower |
| `scalar` | 4 of 5 | 0.9% – 1.2% slower |
| `rust-simd` | 0 of 5 | — |

**Thirteen false positives in twenty checks, on code that had not changed.** The test was not
wrong: the machine's throughput drifts by a percent or two between one moment and the next, and
a test with 32 samples on each side detects that drift as significant every time.

So a regression now has to be **both** statistically significant **and** practically significant,
and the practical threshold is set from that measurement: **5%**, above the observed drift of
0.9%–3.9%. With it, five consecutive runs on unchanged code produce **zero** failures, and the
differences that remain are reported as *within the noise* rather than hidden.

The gate has four answers — no regression, within the noise, regression, and **inconclusive** —
and the last one is not a pass. It says the gate could not tell, which is more useful than a pass
that was decided by a threshold nobody examined.

**And the gate was green while this campaign ran.** It measured `cpp-simd` at **8.628 G rows/s**
against a stored baseline of **8.306 G**, and `scalar` at **3.532 G** against **3.469 G** — above
the baseline in both cases, where the set this campaign replaced ran with every flavour **46–53%
slower** than that same baseline. The baselines are the medians of the stored samples in
`bench-results/kernel-baseline.json`, and the gate's own readings for this campaign are in
[The conditions](#the-conditions). The gate is what says the machine was not thrashing while this
file's figures were taken, and it is why they are about 40% faster than the set they replace.

## 6. Reproducing this

```bash
just dataset       # 6,000,000 rows, deterministic, about 2.8 seconds
just bench         # the kernel A/B -> bench-results/kernel.json
just compare       # DuckDB and Polars  -> bench-results/compare.json

just check-x86     # compile the x86-64 half of the kernel, from this ARM64 Mac
just verify-avx2   # run the AVX2 path under Rosetta 2 and check it against the oracle

python3 scripts/medians.py   # the published table, derived from the committed reports

just perf-gate      # the performance gate: measure, and compare against the baseline
just perf-baseline  # rewrite the baseline from a fresh measurement
```

`just compare` needs `DuckDB` on `PATH` or in `tools/bin/`, and the `polars` Python package
installed. Both versions are recorded in the report.

**A recipe's arguments are positional**, in the order the recipe declares them: `just` assigns the
first argument to the first parameter, and it does not read `name=value` as an assignment. A recipe
that takes a file and an output takes the file first.

**The row-group files of §3j are made by the same generator with one flag different:**

```bash
just dataset                       # the published file: six row groups
just dataset-row-groups 500000     # twelve row groups
just dataset-row-groups 250000     # twenty-four row groups

just sweep "data/lineitem-rg250k.parquet" "q6" "1,2,4,6,8,14" \
    "3" "cold" "bench-results/scaling-rg24-run1.json"
just probe "data/lineitem-rg250k.parquet" "bench-results/row-groups-24-run1.json"
```

**And the two instruments spec 016 added**, one per file, three campaigns each:

```bash
just probe-cost       "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/row-group-cost-6-run1.json"
just probe-batch-size "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/batch-size-run1.json"
```

`probe-cost` is the per-row-group cost probe: the crate's own read of each column, the engine's
scan, the page shape of every row group, and the ladder of reader batch sizes. `probe-batch-size`
sweeps the reader's batch **limit** over the two shapes the lazy path serves and A/Bs the batch
the tree used against the batch it derives now. The `row-group-cost-*` reports are the source of
the ladder and the attribution in [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs), and the
`batch-size-*` reports are the source of the two-shape table in
[§3e](#3e-the-scans-decomposition-and-what-the-compaction-was-worth).

Three campaigns of each, interleaved, and then `python3 scripts/medians.py` derives both tables.
The Polars driver needs an interpreter with `polars` in it: set `PYTHON_BIN` if `python3` is not
the one that has it.

The comparison harness measures the load and the query separately, takes the **median** of the
timed runs, and **cross-checks every answer against the engine's own**. The `SUM` over 555,594
rows differs in the last bits between systems — floating-point addition is not associative and
the engines sum in different orders — so the check uses a relative tolerance rather than exact
equality.

To publish a table, run `just compare` at least three times, keep the reports, and derive the
medians with `scripts/medians.py`. The three reports behind the table in §2 are committed as
`bench-results/compare-run1.json`, `compare-run2.json` and `compare-run3.json`.

**Four more sets of reports sit beside them**, and every figure this file publishes is derived from
one of the five:

```bash
# the set this file publishes now
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-run*.json

# the set before it -- the code the increment replaced, on this same host, back to back
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-before-run*.json

# the set this one replaces
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec026-run*.json

# the set before that one
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec024-run*.json

# the set the code before spec 026 shipped was measured from
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec025-run*.json
```

**And the re-measurement of §2i sits beside them too**, under its own names: the code this increment
ships, and the code it replaces, measured back to back on this host.

```bash
# this increment's set
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec034-run*.json

# the code this increment replaces, on this same host, back to back
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec034-before-run*.json
```

The third is what makes the published **old-and-new** figures checkable rather than typed, and the
guard checks all of them:

```bash
python3 scripts/check-published-figures.py
```

And the instrument that took the hot paths apart has its own two reports — the old code and the new
code, measured back to back on the same host:

```bash
cargo run --release -p columnar-engine --example operator_probe -- \
    --data data/lineitem.parquet --build data/orders.parquet \
    --out bench-results/operator-probe-after.json
```

**And the instrument that took the query apart** — the whole query, the plan output's own stage table,
and the time between them — is spec 024's, and it has two sets of reports as well:

```bash
just probe-query "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/query-cost-run1.json"

just probe-sort "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/sort-cost-run1.json"

just probe-join  "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-run1.json"
```

**And the join probe's re-measurement of §3p has its own two sets**, under their own names, and
`scripts/medians.py` prints them beside the others:

```bash
# this increment's set
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec034-run1.json"

# the code this increment replaces, on this same host, back to back
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec034-before-run1.json"
```

**And §3q's ladder is printed by the same script**, over the set this file publishes and the set spec
034 measured beside it — the leaves' drifts, their movements, the reconciliation and the cost's own
share — and every figure it prints is checked, inside the section that publishes it, by the guard:

```bash
python3 scripts/medians.py
python3 scripts/check-published-figures.py
```

**And §3r's file-state floor has its own three sets**, under their own names, because the instrument
changed — the build side's read and the machine's own measure are measured **in the iteration** now:

```bash
# this increment's set
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec036-run1.json"

# the code this increment replaces, on this same host, back to back
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec036-before-run1.json"

# the hunt: nine more campaigns of the same instrument
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec036-hunt-run1.json"
```

**And the write-side probe**, in the two batch sizes, three campaigns each:

```bash
just probe-write "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/write-cost-run1.json" 65536
just probe-write "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/write-cost-engine-batch-run1.json" 125000
```

The probe writes the published file's own rows back out, commits the file the way an append-only store
commits it, reads it back through the engine's own reader, and compares it with the original byte for
byte. The third argument is the batch size the rows arrive in, and it is the variable: `65536` reproduces
the published file exactly, and the engine's own `125000` does not. The tables in
[§3s](#3s-the-write-side) are derived from those reports by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard
checks them like the rest.

**And the store's own probe**, three campaigns:

```bash
just probe-store "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/store-write-run1.json"
```

The probe writes the published file's own rows **through the store** -- the writer, the manifest and the
commit -- and compares the file the store wrote with the published file **byte for byte**. The tables in
[§3t](#3t-the-stores-own-write) are derived from those reports by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the
guard checks them like the rest.

**And the file boundary's probe**, three campaigns:

```bash
just probe-many-files "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/many-files-run1.json"
```

The probe writes the published file's own **six row groups** as six files, and reads the same rows **as
one file and as six**, through the engine's own reader, in the same campaign. The tables in
[§3u](#3u-the-file-boundarys-cost) are derived from those reports by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the
guard checks them like the rest.

**And the source's probe**, three campaigns:

```bash
just probe-file-set "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/file-set-run1.json"
```

The probe writes the published file's own **six row groups** as six files, and reads the same rows **three
ways**: as the one file, as the six files one by one, and as the six files **read as one table** through the
engine's new source. The tables in [§3v](#3v-the-source-over-a-set-of-files) are derived from those reports
by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks them like the rest.

The probe runs the engine's **own** plans through the engine's own entry point and checks the answers
against the published ones before it times anything, so it is the same query the tables above publish.
Its report is **per query**: each query keeps its own shapes, its own whole-query time and its own
stage table. The tables in
[§3m](#3m-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name) are derived from those reports by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks them like the rest.

**The plan output is in the campaigns too.** Every campaign records the plan output of every query it
ran — the stage breakdown, the rows each stage was handed and produced, and the memory the engine
estimated — so the stage figures in [§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart) are derived from the
committed reports rather than typed into the prose, and the guard checks them like the rest.

---

## 7. How to read this file

The framing rule for this project is **"kernel X on query Y"**, never "this database versus
DuckDB". A number here is a statement about *one operation on one query on one machine*, and it
is only meaningful next to its conditions.

So: the comparison kernel on the Q6 predicate set is 1.47× faster than the compiler's own
vectorisation of the same loop, and 2.48× faster than a genuinely scalar reference, on an M4 Max
with NEON, single-threaded, over 20 million rows. The engine's filter on the Q6 predicate set
takes 6.7 ms. The engine's lazy scan of the same file, reading the two columns Q6 needs and
pushing the predicates down into the scan, takes **15.4 ms** at one thread — against **33.0 ms**
for DuckDB and **193.6 ms** for Polars — and at six workers it takes **4.0 ms** against DuckDB's
10.0 ms. The lazy path is **2.1× faster than DuckDB** at one thread and 12.6× faster than
Polars, and the reason it was not faster before is measured and named: the pushdown's mask was
handed to the reader as a row filter, and a row filter is a list of 2,775,138 row ranges for
2,178,861 surviving rows, applied one range at a time. The engine keeps the mask now, and the
scan that uses it copies each surviving row **once**.

**And the defect spec 016 found is the per-row-group cost.** The engine's reader used a
fixed batch of 65,536 rows, and that number divides none of the three files' row-group sizes — so
every row group ended on a **partial** batch, and a partial batch costs 0.78 ms. The engine paid
a per-row-group cost that DuckDB, Polars and the DataFusion peer did not pay, and the file with
the most parallelism was the slowest one. The batch size is derived from the file now, and the
engine's one-thread figure is flat across the three files — **15.4, 15.7 and 16.4 ms**, where it
was 19.7, 24.8 and 34.6. See [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs).

**And the defect spec 020 found is the join's hasher.** The join's probe hashed with `SipHash`,
which is built to be resistant to collision attacks — a property a query engine's hash table has no
use for, and a cost it does, because the probe runs once for every row of the probe side. The join
stage went from **219.9 ms** to **117.7 ms** and the whole lazy query from **318.9 ms** to
**199.5 ms**, on the same file, in the same session, with the same answer. See
[§4](#4-the-defects-the-increments-found) and
[ADR-0027](docs/adr/0027-the-engine-joins-and-sorts.md).

**And the defect spec 021 found is the shape of the code, not a missing optimisation.** The join's
table was a `HashMap` over a 32-byte enum where an integer key wanted an array; the key reader
consulted a validity bitmap that carried no information; the sort built an enum per row where a
number would do. The engine's cold `join-orders` is **89.8 ms** — a hash join, with `orders` as the
build side, read inside the clock — against DuckDB's **90.0 ms** on the same two files; the engine's
cold `sort-topk` is **40.1 ms** against DuckDB's **37.0 ms**. The engine is **level** with DuckDB on
the join and **1.08× behind** on the sort, and ahead of Polars on both — by **2.4×** on the join and
by **33×** on the sort. The instrument that found it is committed, every candidate it measures is
checked against the engine's own output row for row before it is timed, and the code's share of the
numbers is measured against the old code on the same host. See
[§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart) and
[ADR-0028](docs/adr/0028-the-hot-paths-taken-apart.md).

**And the defect spec 024 found is an account with a hole in it.** The plan output is the engine's
own account of its work, and on the two queries that name a **join** and a **sort** it left **4.2 ms**
of each query **outside every stage** — the filter's selection expanded into six million row
numbers, 24 MB, before either stage's clock started. A selection that holds every row is **the
range** now, the expansion is **gone rather than renamed**, and the plan output's account closes to
**0.01 ms**. The instrument that found it is committed, it runs the engine's own plans, and it checks
each answer against the published one before it times anything. See
[§3m](#3m-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name) and
[ADR-0031](docs/adr/0031-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name.md).

And the eager read — the file → queryable load, which was the largest number in the repository —
is now **527.8 ms** against DuckDB's **1370.0 ms** on the same file at the same thread count. It is the one measurement in this file that is about the *file* rather
than the *query*, and it is swept like the other two. See [§2g](#2g-the-load-sweep--where-the-engines-biggest-number-was).
**Every absolute figure in this paragraph is this set's**, and this set was measured on a host
that was busy at the start and quieter at the end — see
[The conditions](#the-conditions). The ratios are
what the file claims, and they hold.
